## Claude · 2026-04-25T19:34:16.229Z

Saved at [SCSP_ARMY_PAPERWORK_PAIN_RESEARCH.md](SCSP_ARMY_PAPERWORK_PAIN_RESEARCH.md). Here's the strategic read.

## How the Army actually fills out forms (the workflow, not the form)

A single DA-31 leave request takes 6 steps and routinely takes **30+ days** for routine leave: open the PDF (which often gets re-flattened by mail clients), manually retype name/SSN/rank/dates, route through squad leader → platoon sergeant → 1SG → company commander → S1, S1 verifies leave balance in IPPS-A, common rejections (missing signature, wrong dates, balance mismatch) kick it back to start. **And that's the simple form.** TDY is dramatically worse because that's where DTS lives.

## The biggest paperwork pain points (ranked)

**The killer stat to memorize for the pitch:**

> **RAND Corporation: Army company leaders work 12.5-hour days — longer than 96% of all American workers — and less than one-third of that time is on actual unit readiness.**

> **Modern War Institute (West Point): Companies submit "three to four dozen monthly reports." Completion consumes "one week every month for company command teams."**

**The systems that hurt most:**

1. **DTS (Defense Travel System)** — universally hated. Former Col. Arnold Strong calls it the *"Don't Travel Service."* ~18% of vouchers rejected (FY 2004 GAO). Soldiers' personal credit scores get damaged from late government-card payments. One sergeant went 10 months without per diem. **MyTravel replacement promised by 2025, still not fully live.**

2. **IPPS-A (Integrated Personnel and Pay System)** — multi-year delayed. Maintains a public **"Known Issues" PDF updated 24 Dec 2025** — admission that bugs are persistent. Inaccurate retirement dates, missing training records.

3. **Property accountability** — the captain's-rank rant on Reddit: *"Your platoon leadership will join hands and dance on your grave"* over property issues. PBO is the *"Finger of Death."* Supply Sergeant *"will burn your BOMs in a pagan equinox revel."*

4. **NCOERs / OERs** — high-stakes, narrow format, kicked back from HRC for trivial errors, restart entire approval chain. Milnerva exists ($10/mo, 20K users) but is text-only and doesn't fill PDFs.

5. **Other named systems pulling time:** GCSS-Army (logistics), DTMS (training), MEDPROS (medical readiness), ARIMS (records). Modern War Institute: *"troops-to-task reports rarely feed actual decisions, but pull platoon sergeants away from warfighting."*

## What GenAI.mil actually is (and where it leaves a gap)

**Status Apr 2026:** 1.2M+ users; hosts Gemini + Grok + ChatGPT + Claude; IL5/CUI certified; 100K+ agents created (DefenseScoop, Apr 23). Used to draft NCOERs, awards, counseling, memos, S2 intel summaries, training scenarios.

**Critical limitations** (Small Wars Journal, Jan 2026):
- Hallucinations
- Sycophancy ("AI-induced psychosis" for isolated leaders)
- No persistent context — chatbot only knows what user pastes
- **Senior leaders adopt faster than juniors** — exactly inverse of where the pain is
- **Web-only, desk-only**

**The Pentagon's own quote that should drive your design** (DefenseScoop):
> *"We have a lot of mechanics, we got a lot of people turning wrenches. **Not everyone is sitting in a nice, cushy, air-conditioned office typing away at a computer all day.**"*

GenAI.mil is built for desk workers. The pain is at the rank-and-file who don't have a desk. **They need voice. They need offline (motor pool, field exercise, FOB). That's the unfilled lane.**

## My recommendation: pick the junior-NCO TDY persona

| Why | What |
|---|---|
| Most universal pain | Every soldier travels |
| Cleanest 5-min demo | One voice request → two forms out (DA-31 + DD-1351-2) |
| Best dataset alignment | JTR + GSA APIs are public per the SCSP brief |
| Best Problem-Solution Fit score | Persona is so specific judges will see it instantly |
| Best National Impact framing | Every TDY across DoD touches DTS — 3M users × 6hrs/wk × $25/hr = **$23.4B/yr** in recovered labor |

**Build forms in scope: DA-31 (leave) + DD-1351-2 (TDY voucher) + DA-4856 (counseling). Three forms, one persona, end-to-end. Don't try to do all 4 example directions from the SCSP brief.**

## What the Boston judges actually care about

- **Dr. Sanjeev Mohindra** (MIT Lincoln Lab AI Tech Group lead, PhD Cornell). Runs CDAO-funded AI test & evaluation. **He will probe for hallucinations and sycophancy.** Cite "AR 600-8-10, paragraph 4-3" verbatim, show source quote on screen, have a deliberately-out-of-corpus question ready that the system refuses.
- **Dr. Ho-Chit Liu** — Boston co-judge, likely also Lincoln Lab.
- **Stuart Wagner** (DC, also judging) — Air Force/Space Force CDTO who built NIPRGPT before it got phased out. Founded BRAVO Hackathon. Believes in operational prototypes "10x-100x lower cost than DoD's normal pathway." **The voice-first + offline angle directly addresses the gap he saw with NIPRGPT (browser-only).**

## The pitch opener (memorize this)

> **"Army company leaders work 12.5-hour days — longer than 96% of all American workers — and less than a third of that time is on readiness. Why? Companies submit three to four dozen reports a month. GenAI.mil rolled out to 1.2M users in December, but the people who need it most — the mechanics, the platoon sergeants, the soldiers in motor pools where the wifi cuts out — don't have desks. So we built Adjutant: voice-first, fully offline, cites the regulation by section, fills the actual DA-31 PDF. Watch this — [pull wifi cable]."**

Want me to start the SCSP team-registration email + GitHub repo scaffolding next?

## Claude · 2026-04-25T19:56:34.907Z

# Army acronym primer (no military background assumed)

## The acronyms in our research, plain English

### Travel & duty
- **TDY** — *Temporary Duty.* When a soldier is sent away from their home base for a short trip — usually for training, a conference, a meeting, or a temporary mission. Think "business travel" in civilian terms. Trips are typically a few days to a few months. After the trip, they return to their permanent home base.
- **PCS** — *Permanent Change of Station.* Moving from one base to another permanently (every 2-4 years on average). Massively bigger paperwork than TDY.
- **DTS** — *Defense Travel System.* The website soldiers use to book TDY travel and file for reimbursement. Universally hated. The "TurboTax for military travel" — except the IRS actually works.
- **JTR** — *Joint Travel Regulations.* The 1,000+ page rulebook that says how much the military will reimburse for hotels, meals, mileage, etc. The "tax code" of military travel.
- **Per diem** — Daily allowance for meals + lodging while on TDY. Currently $110/day lodging + $68/day meals (FY 2026 default rates). Higher in expensive cities (NYC, DC, San Francisco — set by GSA).
- **GSA** — *General Services Administration.* The federal agency that publishes per-diem rates by city. Their public API is in the SCSP datasets list.
- **DD-1351-2** — The DoD travel voucher. The form you file *after* a TDY trip to get reimbursed for hotels/meals/mileage.

### Forms (the "DA" prefix means "Department of the Army")
- **DA-31** — Request for leave (vacation). The most-touched form in the Army. Soldier uses it to ask "can I take 10 days off starting June 3?"
- **DA-4856** — *Developmental Counseling Form.* Required basically every time a leader sits down with a soldier to discuss performance, problems, goals — anything formal. Routine and constant.
- **DA-2823** — *Sworn Statement.* Used after almost any incident — accident, allegation, witness account.
- **DA-3349** — *Physical Profile.* Issued by medical to limit duty (e.g., "no running, no rucking" while injured).
- **DA-705** — Army Combat Fitness Test scorecard.
- **DA-5500** — Body fat worksheet (when someone exceeds height/weight standards).
- **DD Form** — Same as DA but DoD-wide (used by all branches, not just Army). DD-1351-2 (travel voucher) and DD-2624 (urinalysis chain of custody) are the most common.

### Personnel & pay
- **S1** — The personnel/admin officer at a battalion or unit. The "HR person." They process leave forms, handle promotions, fix pay errors. The bottleneck for almost all admin.
- **G-1** — Same job but at higher level (division/corps/Army-wide). G-1 = senior Army personnel chief.
- **HRC** — *Human Resources Command.* Big Army HQ that owns careers, assignments, evaluations. The kicker-back of NCOERs.
- **IPPS-A** — *Integrated Personnel and Pay System – Army.* The unified HR + payroll system. Combines what used to be eMILPO + a separate pay system. Has a public "known issues" list — that's how broken it is.
- **eMILPO** — *Electronic Military Personnel Office.* Legacy personnel database. Mostly replaced by IPPS-A but some units still touch it.
- **LES** — *Leave and Earnings Statement.* The military pay stub. Shows leave balance, deductions, allowances.
- **ATAP** — *Army Talent Alignment Process.* The "marketplace" where officers and units pick each other for the next assignment. Like a draft, with software.
- **NCOER** — *Noncommissioned Officer Evaluation Report.* Annual performance review for sergeants. Career-defining, format-strict, takes hours to write per soldier.
- **OER** — *Officer Evaluation Report.* Same idea but for officers (lieutenants and up).

### Ranks (the personas)
- **NCO** — *Noncommissioned Officer.* Sergeants. The middle layer. They run the day-to-day. *"The backbone of the Army."*
- **Junior NCO** — Sergeant (E-5) or Staff Sergeant (E-6). 3-10 years in. Most likely to be the demo persona for the hackathon.
- **1SG** — *First Sergeant.* The senior NCO of a company (~120 soldiers). Owns the company's daily operations.
- **PSG** — *Platoon Sergeant.* Senior NCO of a platoon (~30 soldiers).
- **CO / Company Commander** — A captain (officer rank O-3) running a company. The Reddit rant was from one of these. Dies under property accountability paperwork.
- **XO** — *Executive Officer.* The captain's #2. A lieutenant (O-2). Often the property-and-paperwork lead.
- **PBO** — *Property Book Officer.* The terrifying figure who tracks every piece of equipment a unit owns. The "Finger of Death" in the captain's rant.

### Doctrine & logistics
- **AR** — *Army Regulation.* A rulebook that governs a specific topic. AR 600-8-10 = leave rules. AR 670-1 = uniforms. AR 623-3 = evaluations.
- **FM** — *Field Manual.* "How to do" doctrine — leadership, tactics, weapons systems. FM 6-22 = leadership.
- **ADP** — *Army Doctrine Publication.* High-level doctrine documents (the "constitutions" — broader than FMs).
- **DA Pam** — *Department of the Army Pamphlet.* Companion guides to ARs. Less authoritative than the AR but more practical.
- **CCIR** — *Commander's Critical Information Requirements.* The list of things a commander wants to be told about immediately (e.g., a serious injury, a security incident). Drives constant reporting up the chain.
- **CONUS / OCONUS** — *Continental US / Outside Continental US.* "Stationed in Hawaii" = OCONUS. Affects per-diem rates dramatically.
- **FOB** — *Forward Operating Base.* Smaller, deployed bases overseas. Often have spotty internet — exactly why offline AI matters.

### Systems (the "alphabet soup" Modern War Institute called out)
- **DTS** — Defense Travel System (above).
- **GCSS-Army** — *Global Combat Support System – Army.* The logistics + supply + maintenance + property system. Tracks every Humvee, every wrench, every pair of boots.
- **DTMS** — *Digital Training Management System.* Tracks who's been trained on what. Required for every individual + collective training event.
- **MEDPROS** — *Medical Protection System.* Tracks individual medical readiness — vaccines, dental, hearing tests.
- **ARIMS** — *Army Records Information Management System.* Records retention.

### Programs
- **SHARP** — *Sexual Harassment / Assault Response and Prevention.* Required briefings, reporting cycles, investigations. Generates a lot of forms (DA-2823 sworn statements, etc.).
- **EO** — *Equal Opportunity.* Discrimination prevention program. Same general shape as SHARP — required briefings + reporting.
- **ASAP** — *Army Substance Abuse Program.* Includes urinalysis (drug testing). Requires monthly random testing of 10% of unit, quarterly 25%, max 40% per collection. Each test = chain-of-custody paperwork (DD-2624).
- **UPL** — *Unit Prevention Leader.* The soldier in each unit who runs urinalysis. Heavy paperwork job.

### Pay & benefits
- **BAH** — *Basic Allowance for Housing.* Monthly housing stipend, varies by location, rank, dependents.
- **BAS** — *Basic Allowance for Subsistence.* Monthly food allowance.
- **TLE / TLA** — *Temporary Lodging Expense / Allowance.* Hotel reimbursement when between assignments.

### IT and AI specifically
- **GenAI.mil** — The Pentagon's new generative AI platform (launched Dec 2025), 1.2M users, hosts Gemini + Grok + ChatGPT + Claude.
- **CDAO** — *Chief Digital and Artificial Intelligence Office.* The Pentagon office that runs GenAI.mil. Mohindra's research is funded by them.
- **CamoGPT** — Army's purpose-built chatbot, 75K users, predates GenAI.mil.
- **NIPRGPT** — Air Force chatbot built by Stuart Wagner's team. Phased out / Army-blocked.
- **Ask Sage** — Commercial AI vendor that won the $49M IDIQ for Army Enterprise LLM Workspace.
- **NIPR / SIPR / JWICS** — *Non-classified IP Router / Secret IP Router / Joint Worldwide Intelligence Communications System.* Three networks at increasing classification levels. Most things you build live on NIPR (unclassified).
- **CUI / IL5** — *Controlled Unclassified Information / Impact Level 5.* The security tier most DoD work happens at. GenAI.mil is certified for this.
- **CAC** — *Common Access Card.* Military ID with a chip. Required to log into almost any DoD system.
- **CONOPS** — *Concept of Operations.* The "how the system will actually be used" doc. Judges will ask for yours.

### Hackathon-relevant terminology
- **Rank-and-file** — Lower-ranked enlisted soldiers and junior NCOs. The "regular soldiers" — not officers, not senior leaders. SCSP's brief explicitly says you're building for them.
- **Bureaucratic tail** — All the support/admin work that doesn't directly fight wars. The opposite of "tooth" (combat). SCSP's brief uses "administrative trenches" interchangeably.
- **Mission readiness** — Whether a unit can actually deploy and fight. The thing paperwork is supposedly hurting.
- **DDIL** — *Denied, Degraded, Intermittent, Limited.* Describes austere comms environments where offline AI matters. EdgeRunner's whole pitch.

---

## The 10 you actually need to fluently use in the pitch

If you only memorize ten:

1. **TDY** = temporary duty trip
2. **DTS** = the travel-booking website soldiers hate
3. **JTR** = the travel rulebook
4. **DA-31** = leave request form
5. **DD-1351-2** = travel voucher
6. **NCO** = sergeant
7. **S1** = the unit HR officer
8. **AR 600-8-10** = the leave regulation (your demo cites this)
9. **GenAI.mil** = the new Pentagon AI platform you're building "alongside"
10. **EdgeRunner** = the offline-military-AI startup that's your peer-not-competitor

## Claude · 2026-04-25T20:08:06.614Z

## Diagnosis: Hetzner has cut you off — it's not a routing issue, it's the server

Here's what I found:

| Test | Result | What it means |
|---|---|---|
| `ping 136.243.8.51` | 100% packet loss | Server is not responding to ICMP |
| `ssh root@136.243.8.51` (port 22) | Connection timeout | SSH is blocked / down |
| `nc -z 136.243.8.51 22 / 80 / 443 / 8000` | All timed out | **Every port on the IP is unreachable** |
| `https://sabi.eduforequality.org` | **HTTP 200 ✅** | Vercel-hosted (frontend), unaffected |
| `https://api.eduforequality.org/health` | Timeout (15s) | The Hetzner-hosted API is down |
| DNS for `api.eduforequality.org` | resolves to **136.243.8.51** | DNS is fine; the box itself is offline |

**Diagnosis:** Hetzner has **suspended the server** for non-payment. Classic suspension pattern — the IP is still allocated to your account (DNS still points to it, no NXDOMAIN), but every TCP port is firewalled off at their edge. This is exactly what they do before reclaiming hardware. They don't wipe the disk immediately — usually 2–7 day grace before deletion.

**What's safe right now:**
- The Vercel frontend (`sabi.eduforequality.org`) is untouched — different infrastructure, billed via Vercel, still serving 200s.
- DNS records (Cloudflare) are untouched.
- Your Supabase database is untouched.
- Your local code, your GitHub repos, your lesson scripts, your dossiers — all unaffected.

**What's at risk:**
- Server-side files (Chatterbox model weights, Whisper model, your fine-tune attempts in `whisper-finetune/`, `voice_twilio.py`, the asterisk configs, audio cache, log history). All recoverable via `git pull` once back online — anything you didn't push to `thegirwhocodes/sabi-server` is at risk.

**Fix in the next 30 minutes:**

1. Log into [robot.hetzner.com](https://robot.hetzner.com) (or [accounts.hetzner.com](https://accounts.hetzner.com))
2. Look for a red banner / unpaid invoice. Pay it.
3. Reactivation is usually automatic within 5–60 minutes once payment posts.
4. If you don't have funds right now, **email** support@hetzner.com with your customer number, ask for a 7-day grace period — they grant it routinely for first-time misses, and crucially this **prevents data deletion** even if the server stays suspended.

**For the SCSP hackathon:** zero blocker. Adjutant is designed to run **fully on your laptop** — no Hetzner involved. The only Sabi piece you'd reuse is the Chatterbox voice clone for the demo TTS, and even that's optional (system TTS via pyttsx3 is the default fallback, already coded in `adjutant/tts.py`).

Want me to draft the Hetzner support email asking for grace + data preservation? Or pause that and pick back up on Adjutant once you've checked the invoice?

it's saturday - explain this to me in plain english terms 

How the Army actually fills out forms (the workflow, not the form)
A single DA-31 leave request takes 6 steps and routinely takes 30+ days for routine leave: open the PDF (which often gets re-flattened by mail clients), manually retype name/SSN/rank/dates, route through squad leader → platoon sergeant → 1SG → company commander → S1, S1 verifies leave balance in IPPS-A, common rejections (missing signature, wrong dates, balance mismatch) kick it back to start. And that's the simple form. TDY is dramatically worse because that's where DTS lives.

The biggest paperwork pain points (ranked)
The killer stat to memorize for the pitch:

RAND Corporation: Army company leaders work 12.5-hour days — longer than 96% of all American workers — and less than one-third of that time is on actual unit readiness.

Modern War Institute (West Point): Companies submit "three to four dozen monthly reports." Completion consumes "one week every month for company command teams."

The systems that hurt most:

DTS (Defense Travel System) — universally hated. Former Col. Arnold Strong calls it the "Don't Travel Service." ~18% of vouchers rejected (FY 2004 GAO). Soldiers' personal credit scores get damaged from late government-card payments. One sergeant went 10 months without per diem. MyTravel replacement promised by 2025, still not fully live.

IPPS-A (Integrated Personnel and Pay System) — multi-year delayed. Maintains a public "Known Issues" PDF updated 24 Dec 2025 — admission that bugs are persistent. Inaccurate retirement dates, missing training records.

Property accountability — the captain's-rank rant on Reddit: "Your platoon leadership will join hands and dance on your grave" over property issues. PBO is the "Finger of Death." Supply Sergeant "will burn your BOMs in a pagan equinox revel."

NCOERs / OERs — high-stakes, narrow format, kicked back from HRC for trivial errors, restart entire approval chain. Milnerva exists ($10/mo, 20K users) but is text-only and doesn't fill PDFs.

Other named systems pulling time: GCSS-Army (logistics), DTMS (training), MEDPROS (medical readiness), ARIMS (records). Modern War Institute: "troops-to-task reports rarely feed actual decisions, but pull platoon sergeants away from warfighting."

What GenAI.mil actually is (and where it leaves a gap)
Status Apr 2026: 1.2M+ users; hosts Gemini + Grok + ChatGPT + Claude; IL5/CUI certified; 100K+ agents created (DefenseScoop, Apr 23). Used to draft NCOERs, awards, counseling, memos, S2 intel summaries, training scenarios.

Critical limitations (Small Wars Journal, Jan 2026):

Hallucinations
Sycophancy ("AI-induced psychosis" for isolated leaders)
No persistent context — chatbot only knows what user pastes
Senior leaders adopt faster than juniors — exactly inverse of where the pain is
Web-only, desk-only
The Pentagon's own quote that should drive your design (DefenseScoop):

"We have a lot of mechanics, we got a lot of people turning wrenches. Not everyone is sitting in a nice, cushy, air-conditioned office typing away at a computer all day."

GenAI.mil is built for desk workers. The pain is at the rank-and-file who don't have a desk. They need voice. They need offline (motor pool, field exercise, FOB). That's the unfilled lane.

My recommendation: pick the junior-NCO TDY persona
Why	What
Most universal pain	Every soldier travels
Cleanest 5-min demo	One voice request → two forms out (DA-31 + DD-1351-2)
Best dataset alignment	JTR + GSA APIs are public per the SCSP brief
Best Problem-Solution Fit score	Persona is so specific judges will see it instantly
Best National Impact framing	Every TDY across DoD touches DTS — 3M users × 6hrs/wk × $25/hr = $23.4B/yr in recovered labor
Build forms in scope: DA-31 (leave) + DD-1351-2 (TDY voucher) + DA-4856 (counseling). Three forms, one persona, end-to-end. Don't try to do all 4 example directions from the SCSP brief.

What the Boston judges actually care about
Dr. Sanjeev Mohindra (MIT Lincoln Lab AI Tech Group lead, PhD Cornell). Runs CDAO-funded AI test & evaluation. He will probe for hallucinations and sycophancy. Cite "AR 600-8-10, paragraph 4-3" verbatim, show source quote on screen, have a deliberately-out-of-corpus question ready that the system refuses.
Dr. Ho-Chit Liu — Boston co-judge, likely also Lincoln Lab.
Stuart Wagner (DC, also judging) — Air Force/Space Force CDTO who built NIPRGPT before it got phased out. Founded BRAVO Hackathon. Believes in operational prototypes "10x-100x lower cost than DoD's normal pathway." The voice-first + offline angle directly addresses the gap he saw with NIPRGPT (browser-only).
The pitch opener (memorize this)
"Army company leaders work 12.5-hour days — longer than 96% of all American workers — and less than a third of that time is on readiness. Why? Companies submit three to four dozen reports a month. GenAI.mil rolled out to 1.2M users in December, but the people who need it most — the mechanics, the platoon sergeants, the soldiers in motor pools where the wifi cuts out — don't have desks. So we built Adjutant: voice-first, fully offline, cites the regulation by section, fills the actual DA-31 PDF. Watch this — [pull wifi cable]."

## Claude · 2026-04-25T20:10:46.908Z

# Plain-English version: what we know about Army paperwork pain (and why Adjutant matters)

## How a soldier actually files a leave form (the real workflow)

Imagine you're a sergeant. Your sister's getting married next month. You want 10 days off.

To get those 10 days, here's what actually happens:

1. **You open a PDF.** It's called the **DA-31** — that's just the form number for "Request and Authority for Leave." You download it from the Army's website.
2. **You manually retype your info.** Name, your military ID, rank, unit, the dates, where you'll be staying, an emergency contact. Even though the Army already knows all of this, you type it from scratch.
3. **You print it.** Yes, in 2026. Because the digital form sometimes "flattens" when you email it (becomes a picture instead of a fillable form), so people just print it.
4. **You pass it up the chain.** First your squad leader signs it. Then your platoon sergeant. Then the First Sergeant (the senior enlisted guy in your company). Then the Company Commander (a captain). Then it lands on the desk of the **S1** — that's the unit's HR office.
5. **The S1 has to verify your leave balance.** They open a separate computer system called **IPPS-A** (the Army's HR/payroll software, famously buggy — it has a public "Known Issues" page that they update every few weeks because the bugs are so persistent).
6. **The form gets rejected for something tiny.** Missing a signature. Wrong date format. Leave-balance number doesn't match the LES (your pay stub). The whole thing comes back to you. **Restart at step 1.**

For something that should take 5 minutes, this routinely takes **30 days** of back-and-forth. **And that's the easy form.**

**TDY** (a temporary work trip — like getting sent to a 5-day training course at another base) is way worse, because TDY goes through a system called **DTS** (Defense Travel System). DTS is so universally hated that a former Army colonel publicly nicknamed it the *"Don't Travel Service."* About 1 in 5 travel reimbursements gets rejected and has to be resubmitted. One sergeant in a news report went **10 months** without getting reimbursed for a trip — which damaged his personal credit score because the government travel credit card kept charging him in the meantime.

---

## The two stats that prove this isn't just complaining

These are the numbers we'll quote at the judges:

**1. RAND Corporation study:**
Army company leaders (the captains running ~120-soldier units) work an average **12.5-hour day** — that's longer than 96% of all American workers. **Less than one-third** of that time is actually spent on training their soldiers or making the unit ready to fight. The rest is paperwork.

**2. Modern War Institute (the Army's own think tank at West Point):**
A typical Army company has to submit **three to four dozen monthly reports**. Just doing the reports eats up *one full week of every month* for the company's leadership team.

So 70% of an Army officer's day is paperwork. That's our enemy. That's the problem we're solving.

---

## The systems that hurt soldiers most (in plain English)

| System | What it is | Why it sucks |
|---|---|---|
| **DTS** | The website you use to book and get reimbursed for work travel | Soldiers say it ruins their credit, rejects vouchers for tiny errors, and bottlenecks at understaffed approval offices |
| **IPPS-A** | The Army's HR + payroll system | Public bug-tracker is so big they update it monthly. Wrong retirement dates, missing training records |
| **Property accountability** | The system that tracks every Humvee, weapon, and pair of boots a unit owns | A captain's viral Reddit rant called the property officer the *"Finger of Death"* — one mistake here can end your career |
| **NCOERs / OERs** | Annual performance reviews for sergeants and officers | Career-defining. A trivial format error sends them back from headquarters and restarts the entire approval chain. There's a $10/month startup called Milnerva that helps people write them — 20,000 users — but it's text-only and doesn't actually fill any PDF |

There are 4 more buried-acronym systems that pull soldiers' time: **GCSS-Army** (logistics), **DTMS** (training tracking), **MEDPROS** (medical readiness), **ARIMS** (records management). Modern War Institute's own quote: *"these reports rarely feed actual decisions — they just pull platoon sergeants away from warfighting."*

---

## What GenAI.mil already is (and where it leaves a gap)

The Pentagon launched **GenAI.mil** in December 2025. As of April 2026:

- **1.2 million+ users**
- Hosts the four big AI models (Google Gemini, OpenAI's ChatGPT, Anthropic's Claude, xAI's Grok)
- Government-secure (the technical term is "IL5/CUI certified")
- People have already built **100,000+ AI agents** on it

What people use it for: writing performance reviews, drafting awards, summarizing policy documents, drafting memos, intel summaries.

**But here's the gap.** The Pentagon's own published quote (December 2025):

> *"We have a lot of mechanics, we got a lot of people turning wrenches. **Not everyone is sitting in a nice, cushy, air-conditioned office typing away at a computer all day.**"*

GenAI.mil is **a website**. You open Chrome. You sign in with your military ID card. You type. That works fine if you're a colonel at the Pentagon. It doesn't work if you're:
- A platoon sergeant in a motor pool with grease on your hands
- A soldier on a forward base where the wifi is unreliable
- A junior NCO with 15 minutes between meetings, not at a desk

The Small Wars Journal also flagged that the AI on GenAI.mil **hallucinates** (makes up regulations that don't exist) and is **sycophantic** (tells you what you want to hear instead of what's true). For paperwork the chain of command is going to sign, that's a real problem.

**The unfilled lane:** voice-first (you talk to it, it talks back). Offline (works without internet). Cites the regulation by section so it can't make things up. **Fills the actual PDF.** Nobody has shipped that.

That's Adjutant.

---

## Why we're picking the "junior NCO planning a trip" persona

Out of every possible Army user, we're building for one specific person: a sergeant (E-5 or E-6, mid-career enlisted) who needs to file leave AND go on a work trip in the same week.

Why this person:

- **Universal pain** — every single soldier travels at some point
- **Cleanest demo** — one voice request can produce two filled forms (the DA-31 leave form + the DD-1351-2 travel voucher)
- **Public datasets** — the Joint Travel Regulations and the GSA per-diem rates are public APIs that the SCSP brief explicitly tells us we can use
- **Specific = memorable** — judges will see exactly who this is for
- **Big number** — 3 million service members × 6 hours per week of paperwork × $25/hour loaded labor cost = **$23.4 billion per year** in recovered productive time

We're scoping to **3 forms only**: the leave form (DA-31), the travel voucher (DD-1351-2), and the counseling form (DA-4856). Three forms, one persona, end-to-end. We're deliberately NOT trying to do all four example directions from the SCSP brief — depth beats breadth in 30 hours.

---

## Who's judging us in Boston

Two judges, both serious AI/defense people:

**Dr. Sanjeev Mohindra** — leads MIT Lincoln Lab's AI Technology Group. PhD from Cornell. His current research is funded by the Pentagon's AI office and is specifically about **how to test AI for reliability in defense applications.** Translation: he's professionally skeptical of AI demos. He will try to break ours.

What this means for us: **every answer must cite its source by document and section number** ("Per AR 600-8-10, paragraph 4-3..."). And we'll deliberately ask Adjutant a question that's NOT in our regulation library — and let the judges see it correctly refuse to guess. That's the "we're not like the other AI demos" moment.

**Dr. Ho-Chit Liu** — Mohindra's co-judge in Boston, similar background.

There's also **Stuart Wagner** (judging in DC, but his name carries weight) — he's the Air Force's Chief Digital Transformation Officer and built **NIPRGPT** (one of the early Pentagon chatbots) before it got phased out. He believes in cheap, fast prototypes. The voice-first + offline angle directly addresses the gap he saw with NIPRGPT (which was browser-only and got blocked by the Army for security reasons).

---

## The pitch opener (memorize this — it's 30 seconds)

> *"Army company leaders work 12.5-hour days — longer than 96% of all American workers — and less than a third of that time is on readiness. Why? Companies submit three to four dozen reports a month. GenAI.mil rolled out to 1.2 million users in December, but the people who need it most — the mechanics, the platoon sergeants, the soldiers in motor pools where the wifi cuts out — don't have desks.*
>
> *So we built Adjutant: voice-first, fully offline, cites the regulation by section, fills the actual DA-31 PDF. Watch this..."*
>
> **[pull wifi cable]**

That's the whole opening. The wifi cable is the kill shot — it visually proves "offline" before you've explained anything.

---

## TL;DR — what's in your head when you walk in

1. **The pain is real.** 12.5-hour days. 30+ days for one leave form. $23B/yr of wasted labor.
2. **GenAI.mil exists** but it's a website for desk workers. The pain is at the rank-and-file who aren't at desks.
3. **Adjutant fills the gap:** voice-first, offline, cites regulations by section, fills real PDFs.
4. **We picked the easiest persona to demo:** a sergeant planning leave + a work trip.
5. **The judges will test for hallucination.** We constrain the AI to cite sources, and we have a deliberate "I don't know" demo ready.
6. **The wifi-disconnect moment is the kill shot.** Pull the cable. Watch it still work.

## Claude · 2026-04-25T20:17:51.520Z

# Plain-English answers — fast

## 1. The single biggest time-suck

**Tracking and reporting things to higher headquarters.** Specifically: reading the question someone above them asked, hunting through 4-6 different computer systems for the answer, copy-pasting it into a spreadsheet or PowerPoint, emailing it up. Then doing that 3-4 dozen times a month.

The Modern War Institute (West Point's think tank) calls these *"troops-to-task reports"* and says they *"rarely feed actual decisions, but pull platoon sergeants away from warfighting."* This is the **#1 daily activity** for the people whose job is admin (S1 personnel clerks, company XOs, platoon sergeants, supply sergeants).

The "writing forms" piece — DA-31s, NCOERs, counseling — comes second. The reporting churn is bigger because it's daily, never-ending, and the data you need lives across IPPS-A + DTS + GCSS-Army + DTMS + MEDPROS + ARIMS, and none of those talk to each other. So you spend 70% of your time **manually moving information between systems** that should be talking to each other but aren't.

That's the actual job description if you're a company XO or an S1 clerk: **human API between broken government databases.**

## 2. What the rank-and-file actually carries

Three things, all separate:

**Personal smartphone (their own, off-duty).** Almost every soldier under 35 has one. iPhone or Android. Used off-duty for everything. **Cannot be used for official paperwork** — no government data is allowed on a personal device. They use it for personal life, and increasingly for things like reading news about their own service.

**A government computer (NIPR laptop or desktop).** Locked-down Windows laptop or shared workstation in the company office. Requires their **CAC** (Common Access Card — military ID with a chip) to log in. **This is where 100% of paperwork happens today.** They wait in line for it.

**A radio (NOT a walkie-talkie).** The military uses tactical radios — SINCGARS, Harris radios, MBITRs. These are voice-only, encrypted, no data, no internet. Designed for combat communications: "Bravo 6, this is Bravo 1, contact east, over." **You cannot fill out a form on a radio.** They don't even use text. They're not a paperwork tool, they're a battle tool.

So no — radios are out. There's no "filling out the form via walkie-talkie" path.

**What this means for Adjutant:** The vehicle is **the government laptop**, but our innovation is making the *interface* on that laptop be voice instead of typing. The soldier sits down at the company computer (the one they currently spend 12 hours a day at), CAC's in, opens Adjutant, and instead of clicking through 47 fields they just talk to it. Same hardware they already have. New interaction model.

The "offline" angle isn't because they're filling forms in the field on a radio — it's because:
- The government laptops on a forward base often have intermittent connectivity
- GenAI.mil specifically requires connectivity (it's hosted in the cloud)
- Some sensitive units are on closed networks (no internet at all)
- The CAC + cloud round-trip is slow

**Adjutant runs locally on that laptop.** No cloud round-trip. Works whether the wifi's up or down.

## TL;DR for your pitch

- **Biggest time-suck:** moving data between government systems that don't talk to each other (~70% of an admin person's day).
- **Hardware they have:** government-issued laptop with their CAC. Personal phone (off-duty only). Combat radios (combat only).
- **Adjutant runs on the laptop.** Voice replaces typing. Stays local. Works whether the network is up or down.

## Claude · 2026-04-25T20:23:19.415Z

# Sharp questions — let me answer each in plain English

## Question 1: Can Adjutant talk to all those databases (IPPS-A, DTS, GCSS-Army, etc.) and auto-fill the form from them?

**Honest answer: not in 30 hours, and probably not in 30 weeks. Here's why — and what to do about it.**

### The real-world barrier

The Pentagon databases (IPPS-A, DTS, GCSS-Army, DTMS, MEDPROS) are not normal SaaS products with public APIs you can hit with a curl request. To talk to them you need:

1. **A government-issued PKI certificate** (essentially a digital ID for your software, signed by a DoD Certificate Authority). Getting one takes 6–18 months of paperwork.
2. **An ATO (Authority to Operate)** — formal certification that your software has passed a security audit. Costs $200K–$2M, takes 12–24 months.
3. **A CAC PIV passthrough** — your software has to authenticate as the user using their physical military ID card.
4. **Network position** — many of these systems only allow connections from certain secure networks (NIPR, SIPR), not from a hackathon laptop.
5. **Inter-system contracts** — IPPS-A doesn't share data with DTS by default. There are formal data-sharing agreements that take legal review.

This is the exact reason the Pentagon spent **billions** building IPPS-A over a decade and it *still* doesn't talk cleanly to DTS. It's not a technical problem they haven't solved — it's an organizational and security problem.

### What we CAN say to judges

> *"The end-state vision is full database integration — Adjutant queries IPPS-A for your leave balance, queries DTMS to confirm you're not in mandatory training during your requested dates, generates the form auto-populated. That's the SIPR-deployed v2 with an Authority to Operate. For this hackathon — 30 hours, public corpus, unclassified — we're proving the front-end interaction model: voice → reg-grounded answer → filled PDF. The integration layer plugs into the same architecture; the data adapters are interchangeable."*

This is actually a STRENGTH in the pitch. You've thought about the real deployment path. Most teams haven't.

### The clever middle-ground (if you want to seem ambitious)

We can fake it for the demo with two specific moves:

1. **A "personnel data" JSON file** that pretends to be an IPPS-A export — name, SSN-last-4, rank, unit, leave balance, BAH zip. Adjutant reads it locally. Tell judges: "this is a stand-in for the IPPS-A REST endpoint that exists in the v2 deployment."
2. **A "calendar" JSON file** pretending to be DTMS — soldier's training schedule. Adjutant checks for conflicts before generating the form. Tells the judge "you have JRTC mission rehearsal July 12–18, that conflicts with your requested leave dates" — which is exactly the kind of cross-system intelligence soldiers want.

Both are 1-hour adds and they make the demo dramatically more impressive. **Do this if we're ahead of schedule by Saturday night.**

---

## Question 2: Why are officers so slow to sign?

This is the most undervalued angle in the pitch. The answer has 4 parts.

### a) The form physically (or digitally) waits in line

Forms today route through **CAC-signed PDFs emailed up the chain** OR **physical paper**. Both have the same problem:

- The soldier emails or hands the form to their **squad leader**. Sits in his inbox.
- He signs (CAC stamp on PDF, or pen on paper) and emails to the **platoon sergeant**. Sits.
- Platoon sergeant → **First Sergeant**. Sits.
- 1SG → **company commander**. Sits.
- Company commander → **S1**. Sits.
- S1 verifies → routes back to commander for final approval. Sits.

Each link in the chain typically takes **1–3 days** because each person has 30 other forms in their queue and they only review on certain days. Five links × 2 days = 10 days for a routine leave form. Two rejection cycles = 30+ days.

### b) They sign late because they're afraid

Officers and senior NCOs are personally accountable for what they sign. If they sign a form with a wrong leave balance and the soldier overdraws, **they** get the AR 15-6 investigation. So they pause. They re-read. They check the LES against IPPS-A. They send it back if anything looks off.

This isn't laziness — it's defensive caution from a system that punishes mistakes much more than it rewards speed.

### c) They lose context across systems

The S1 sees a leave form and has to mentally cross-check:
- Is this soldier in mandatory training those dates? (DTMS)
- Are they on a deployment hold? (IPPS-A)
- Did they go to a profile that limits leave? (MEDPROS)
- Are they in a financial garnishment that affects pay? (DFAS)
- Are they up for promotion / school / PCS in that window? (HRC)

Each of those is a separate login. Most S1s skip half of them and just sign — which is why bad forms slip through, and why the careful S1 takes 4× longer.

### d) The physical paper problem (still real in 2026)

A surprising amount of routine Army paperwork is still printed, hand-signed, scanned, and emailed. Reasons:
- Some commanders don't have CAC readers at home
- Some PDFs from Army Pubs Directorate "flatten" when emailed and lose their fillable fields
- Audit trail: a wet signature with a date stamp is harder to dispute than a digital one
- Habit / generational training: the senior NCOs and field-grade officers came up before digital signing was reliable

So in many units the form literally **walks** down the hall, gets signed, walks back. **A leave form in a deployed brigade can take two weeks because the company commander is in a different tent and only does paperwork after 1900.**

### What this means for Adjutant's pitch

Two angles you should add to the demo:

**Angle 1 — "the chain doesn't change, but the inputs are clean."**
> *"Adjutant doesn't replace the chain of command's authority — squad leader → platoon sergeant → 1SG → commander → S1, that's not changing. What changes is what arrives at each desk. Today the S1 gets a form with a typo in the leave dates and a balance that doesn't match IPPS-A — and they kick it back, 3 days lost. Adjutant generates a form that's already cross-checked against the regulation. Fewer rejections. Faster chain."*

**Angle 2 — "the digital signing layer is the next milestone, not this hackathon."**
> *"The natural follow-on is a CAC-signed PDF generation pipeline so the form is signature-ready out of Adjutant. We deliberately scoped that out for 30 hours because the harder problem is the front-end intelligence — proving the AI can read a request, cite the regulation, fill the form correctly. Add the CAC signing step and you've replaced 80% of the paperwork loop end-to-end."*

---

## Three angles I think we haven't surfaced yet

### Angle A — **The "context-aware refusal" is bigger than we framed it**

We talked about Adjutant refusing when a question is out-of-corpus. The bigger version: **Adjutant refusing because the soldier's request would violate a regulation.**

Example for the demo:
> *"I want to take 35 days of ordinary leave."*

Adjutant should reply: *"Per AR 600-8-10, paragraph 4-3, ordinary leave requests over 30 consecutive days require additional approval from the next higher commander. I'd recommend splitting into two leave events or routing through your battalion commander. I'll draft the form for 30 days and flag the extension."*

That's a reliability moment. It's the difference between an LLM-with-RAG and a paperwork assistant that actually understands the rules. **20-minute add. Big judge moment.**

### Angle B — **Spouse-and-family use case (sleeper)**

A massive amount of Army paperwork involves the soldier's family — TRICARE, BAH, dependents, base housing, school transfers during PCS. Soldier deployed → spouse can't reach them → spouse needs to file something → can't because it requires the soldier's CAC.

Adjutant could be the spouse-side companion: voice-driven Q&A for "what does AR 608-99 say about family support during deployment?" Not for hackathon scope, but it's a *huge* aside if a judge asks "what's the next user."

### Angle C — **The "one prompt, many forms" superpower**

A clever demo move: a single voice request that generates **multiple** forms in parallel.

> *"I'm leaving for JRTC July 14, 5 days. Need to file the TDY voucher, get my counseling done with my squad before I go, and put in for two days of leave when I get back to recover."*

Adjutant generates **three forms at once** — DD-1351-2 for the TDY, DA-4856 counseling, DA-31 for post-trip leave. None of the existing tools (Milnerva, Ask Sage, GenAI.mil chat) do this. **It's the moment that makes you visibly different from a chatbot.**

This is a 1-hour add and probably the biggest "wow" we can build on top of the base. **If we have time Saturday night, do this instead of polish.**

### Angle D — **Reverse use: "what should I be filing right now?"**

Soldiers don't always know what paperwork they OWE. PCS-ing in 90 days? Should have started shipping POV paperwork 60 days out. Going on terminal leave? Should have a final out-processing checklist.

Adjutant could query against AR 600-8-101 (Personnel Processing) and proactively say: *"You PCS to Fort Lewis in 87 days. By day 60 you need to have submitted your POV shipping request, AR 600-8-101 paragraph 3-2. Want me to draft it?"*

That's a productivity moment, not just a paperwork one. **Out of scope for 30 hours, but mention it in the "future work" slide if asked.**

### Angle E — **The data-quality angle (judge bait for Mohindra)**

Dr. Mohindra runs AI test & evaluation. He cares about **measurable reliability.**

Build a tiny eval harness: 20 questions about AR 600-8-10 with ground-truth answers we hand-write. Run them through Adjutant. Show judges: "94% accurate citations, 100% refusal rate on out-of-corpus questions, zero hallucinated paragraph numbers."

Then — and this is the kicker — **run the same 20 questions through GenAI.mil's hosted Gemini and show it hallucinating.** Side-by-side comparison.

That's a 2-hour add. **It's the move that wins Mohindra's vote single-handedly.**

---

## My priority list if you have 4 spare hours Saturday night

| # | Add | Time | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Multi-form generation from one voice request | 1h | Biggest visible "wow" beyond base |
| 2 | Eval harness (20 questions, side-by-side vs GenAI.mil) | 2h | Wins Mohindra |
| 3 | Context-aware refusal (35-day leave example) | 20min | Reliability moment |
| 4 | Fake IPPS-A + DTMS JSON files for cross-system check | 1h | Demonstrates the integration story |
| 5 | "Wifi cable yank" rehearsal × 5 | 30min | Don't fumble the kill shot |

If you have less time, do 3 + 5. They're tiny and they punch hard.

---

## TL;DR

**Q1 (databases):** Real integration is a multi-year ATO/PKI problem we can't solve in 30 hours. **But we can fake it convincingly with local JSON stand-ins** and frame it as "the v2 deployment plugs into IPPS-A — the architecture is ready, the security accreditation isn't this hackathon's job."

**Q2 (officials slow):** Forms wait in 5-link approval chains; signers are personally accountable so they over-verify; cross-system context-checking is exhausting; and paper still moves physically in many units. **Adjutant's role is to clean up the inputs so fewer rejections happen, not to bypass the chain.**

**Hidden angles to consider:** context-aware refusal, multi-form-from-one-prompt, side-by-side eval vs GenAI.mil, proactive "what should I be filing" mode, family/spouse use case.

Want me to write the multi-form-from-one-prompt code now? That's the single highest-impact add.

## Claude · 2026-04-25T20:31:39.785Z

# Adjutant — comprehensive 30-hour build plan

**Now:** Saturday April 25, ~12:00 noon Boston (you're at the venue).
**Submission:** Sunday April 26, 5:00 PM local. GitHub link + README emailed to `hack@scsp.ai`.
**Demo:** Live in front of judges Sunday evening.

---

## Phase 0 — Right now, before anything else (45 min)

This unblocks everything. **Don't skip.**

### 0.1 Confirm the team (15 min)
- [ ] Decide if you're going solo or recruiting onsite. Ideal: find one veteran or active-duty hacker in the room. Walk up, say *"I'm building voice-first offline AI for Army paperwork — I need someone who's filed a DA-31."* You will get a yes within 20 minutes.
- [ ] Backup: pair with one Python/web dev. Even a stranger willing to handle the frontend buys you 6 hours.
- [ ] Solo plan is fine. The repo is structured to be solo-buildable.

### 0.2 Send the registration email (5 min) — **HARD DEADLINE 2:00 PM**

Open `/Users/naomiivie/adjutant/docs/SCSP_REGISTRATION_EMAIL.md`, fill in member names, send to `hack@scsp.ai` with subject `SCSP Hackathon Charlie Mike FINAL`. Done.

### 0.3 Get the laptop ready (15 min)
- [ ] Mac on power. Plug in mouse/external keyboard if you have one — you'll be typing for 24+ hours.
- [ ] Install Ollama: `brew install ollama` (or download from ollama.com)
- [ ] Start the model pull NOW so it runs while you do other setup: `ollama pull llama3.1:8b` (~5GB, ~5–10 min on hackathon wifi)
- [ ] Make sure Python 3.11+ is installed: `python3 --version`

### 0.4 Initialize the GitHub repo (10 min)

```bash
cd /Users/naomiivie/adjutant
git init
git add .
git commit -m "Initial scaffolding: voice-first offline Army paperwork assistant"
gh repo create adjutant --public --source=. --remote=origin --push
```

You need the public URL early because it goes in the registration email.

---

## Phase 1 — Get the core stack running (Sat 1:00 PM – 4:00 PM, 3h)

**Goal:** end of this phase, you can talk to the laptop and get back a regulation-grounded text answer with citations. No PDF yet, no TTS yet.

### 1.1 Install dependencies (15 min)

```bash
cd /Users/naomiivie/adjutant
python3 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
pip install -r requirements.txt
cp .env.example .env
```

### 1.2 Download the corpus (15 min)

```bash
python scripts/download_corpus.py
```

This pulls AR 600-8-10, JTR June 2025, plus the three blank PDFs (DA-31, DD-1351-2, DA-4856). If any download fails (DoD URLs sometimes 403 from non-CAC clients), download manually in browser and drop into `corpus/` and `forms/`.

### 1.3 Build the FAISS index (15 min)

```bash
python scripts/ingest_corpus.py
```

You should see ~500–2,000 chunks indexed. Confirm `.faiss_index/faiss.bin` and `.faiss_index/chunks.pkl` exist.

### 1.4 Smoke test retrieval (15 min)

Open a Python REPL:
```python
from adjutant.rag import retrieve
chunks = retrieve("How does ordinary leave accrue?", top_k=3)
for c in chunks:
    print(c["source"], c["section"], c["text"][:200])
```

You should see chunks from AR 600-8-10, ideally with a paragraph reference. **If retrieval is poor**, increase `CHUNK_SIZE` in `scripts/ingest_corpus.py` to 1200, re-ingest. Or switch the embedder to `sentence-transformers/all-mpnet-base-v2` (better quality, slower).

### 1.5 Smoke test the LLM (15 min)

```bash
ollama serve  # Terminal 1, leave running
```

```python
# Terminal 2 Python REPL
from adjutant.llm import answer_query
from adjutant.rag import retrieve
chunks = retrieve("How does ordinary leave accrue?", top_k=5)
print(answer_query("How does ordinary leave accrue?", chunks))
```

You should get a short paragraph citing AR 600-8-10. **If the LLM is too slow**, drop to `llama3.1:8b-instruct-q4_K_M` (smaller quant, faster) or `llama3.2:3b` (much smaller, faster, slightly dumber).

### 1.6 Run the server (15 min)

```bash
uvicorn adjutant.server:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8000 --reload
```

Open `http://localhost:8000/web/`. You should see the mic button. Hit `/health` and `/forms` to confirm endpoints respond.

### 1.7 First end-to-end voice test (1.5 h)

This is where things break. Common issues:
- **Mic permissions** in browser — Chrome will prompt; allow.
- **Whisper model download** is ~3GB, happens on first call — be patient on first transcription, time it.
- **WebM audio format** sometimes confuses faster-whisper; if transcription returns empty, log the audio bytes and try saving + transcribing manually.
- **Citations come back empty** — that means RAG returned nothing; check the FAISS index path.

By 4:00 PM you should be able to: hold mic, say *"How does leave accrue?"*, see transcript, see grounded answer with citations on screen.

---

## Phase 2 — Form-fill flow (Sat 4:00 PM – 8:00 PM, 4h)

**Goal:** end of this phase, the leave-request demo flow works end-to-end with a real filled DA-31 PDF in the browser.

### 2.1 Extract the PDF schemas (30 min)

```bash
python scripts/extract_form_schemas.py
```

This prints the actual AcroForm field names from the blank PDFs. **They will not match the placeholder names in `adjutant/forms.py`** — Army Pubs PDFs use cryptic names like `topmostSubform[0].Page1[0].LeaveDates[0]`.

Open `forms/extracted_schemas.json`, then update `adjutant/forms.py` so the keys in each form's `fields` dict match the actual PDF field names. **This is fiddly and tedious. Plan 45 min.**

### 2.2 Test PDF fill round-trip (30 min)

```python
from adjutant.pdf_fill import fill_pdf
from adjutant.forms import DA_31

test_data = {
    "name": "CHEN, MAYA L",
    "ssn": "1234",
    "rank": "E-5",
    "unit": "B Co, 1-504 PIR, 82nd ABN",
    "leave_type": "Ordinary",
    "start_date": "2026-06-03",
    "end_date": "2026-06-12",
    "days_requested": "10",
    "leave_address": "1234 Peachtree St, Atlanta GA 30309",
    "leave_phone": "919-555-0144",
    "emergency_contact": "Maria Chen (mother)",
}
fill_pdf(DA_31["pdf_path"], test_data, "test_filled.pdf")
```

Open `test_filled.pdf` in Preview. **If fields are blank**, the field-name keys are wrong — re-check the schema. **If fields show as text but disappear when you click**, set `NeedAppearances` properly (already in `pdf_fill.py`, but some viewers ignore it — ok, it'll print correctly).

### 2.3 Wire LLM extraction into the pipeline (1 h)

The pipeline already exists in `adjutant/server.py` `/query` endpoint. Test it:

```bash
curl -X POST http://localhost:8000/query \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"query": "I need to file ten days of leave starting June 3, going to Atlanta, emergency contact Maria Chen 919-555-0144", "form_id": "DA-31"}'
```

You should get back JSON with `form_data`, `pdf_url`, and a `spoken_summary`. Open the `pdf_url` in browser.

### 2.4 Tighten LLM extraction (1 h)

Common failures here:
- **LLM returns prose instead of JSON** — `format="json"` parameter in `adjutant/llm.py` should force this; if your Ollama version doesn't support it, switch to a stricter system prompt that ends in `"Return ONLY a JSON object."`
- **LLM invents fields not in the schema** — narrow the prompt: include the exact schema dict in the user prompt, say *"Use ONLY these field names."*
- **LLM hallucinates personal info** (a fake SSN, a wrong unit) — strengthen the *"return null for fields you cannot infer"* instruction; add `missing_fields` to the schema.

Test with 5 different leave scenarios. Make sure 5/5 produce a fillable PDF.

### 2.5 Add TTS (1 h)

Test the system fallback first (it's bulletproof, no models to load):

```python
from adjutant.tts import synthesize
synthesize("Per AR 600-8-10, paragraph 4-3, your ten days of leave are approved.", "test.wav")
# Open test.wav, listen.
```

If you have time and want better voice quality: spin up Chatterbox locally (Sabi pattern). 1 hour. Not required — pyttsx3 is fine for the demo if it sounds like a robot, that's actually on-brand for "I built this in 30 hours."

By 8:00 PM you should have: voice → transcribed → RAG → grounded answer with citations → filled DA-31 PDF in iframe → spoken reply.

**Take a break. Eat dinner. 30 min.**

---

## Phase 3 — Add TDY (DD-1351-2) and counseling (DA-4856) flows (Sat 8:30 PM – 11:00 PM, 2.5h)

### 3.1 DD-1351-2 with per-diem math (1.5 h)

The trick here is the per-diem calculation. The LLM can do it but is unreliable on math. **Better approach:** the LLM extracts city/state/days, and `adjutant/per_diem.py` does the actual math deterministically.

Add this to `adjutant/server.py` `/query` route, right after extraction:

```python
# inside the form-fill block, only when target_form == "DD-1351-2":
if target_form == "DD-1351-2" and form_data:
    from adjutant.per_diem import calculate_tdy_total
    from datetime import date
    if form_data.get("tdy_location") and form_data.get("total_days"):
        city, _, state = form_data["tdy_location"].partition(",")
        per_diem = calculate_tdy_total(
            city.strip(), state.strip(), int(form_data["total_days"])
        )
        form_data["lodging_per_day"] = per_diem["lodging_per_day"]
        form_data["mie_per_day"] = per_diem["mie_per_day"]
        form_data["estimated_total"] = per_diem["estimated_total"]
```

Test with the JRTC scenario: *"5 days at Fort Polk starting July 14, home station Fort Bragg."*
Expected: looks up Leesville, LA per-diem ($110/$68), 5 days × correct math, ~$822 total.

### 3.2 DA-4856 counseling form (1 h)

Less math, more language generation. Test scenario: *"counseling for Specialist Garcia for being late to formation twice."*

The LLM should populate `purpose`, `key_points`, `plan_of_action` in proper Army-style bullets. Test that field text doesn't break PDF rendering (long strings can clip).

---

## Phase 4 — The high-impact polish adds (Sat 11:00 PM – Sun 2:00 AM, 3h)

These are the moves that win Novelty + Tech Difficulty + Mohindra's eval brain.

### 4.1 Multi-form from one prompt (1 h) — the "wow" move

Modify `_infer_form` in `server.py` to return a **list** of form IDs, not just one. Then the route iterates and generates each.

```python
def _infer_forms(query: str) -> list[str]:
    q = query.lower()
    forms = []
    if any(k in q for k in ("leave", "vacation", "time off")):
        forms.append("DA-31")
    if any(k in q for k in ("tdy", "travel", "voucher", "trip", "jrtc")):
        forms.append("DD-1351-2")
    if any(k in q for k in ("counsel", "counseling")):
        forms.append("DA-4856")
    return forms
```

Test: *"I'm going to JRTC July 14 for 5 days, need to counsel SPC Garcia tomorrow, and want 2 days of leave when I get back."* → 3 forms generated. **Demo gold.**

### 4.2 Context-aware refusal (20 min)

Add a few rule-based checks in the LLM prompt. Add to `prompts.py`:

```
COMPLIANCE RULES (always apply):

- If user requests >30 consecutive days of ordinary leave, respond:
  "Per AR 600-8-10 paragraph 4-3, leave over 30 days requires next-higher-commander approval. I'll draft for 30 days and flag the extension for your battalion commander."

- If user requests leave during what is clearly a major training event
  (JRTC, NTC, deployment, gunnery), flag the conflict and recommend deconfliction.

- If user requests TDY without a stated purpose, ask for the purpose.
```

Test: *"I want 35 days of leave"* → should get the cited refusal. **This is the moment Mohindra notices.**

### 4.3 Eval harness vs GenAI.mil (1.5 h) — the move that wins Mohindra

Create `tests/eval_questions.json` with 20 questions and ground-truth answers about AR 600-8-10:

```json
[
  {
    "q": "How many days of ordinary leave does an enlisted soldier accrue per month?",
    "ground_truth": "2.5 days/month per AR 600-8-10 paragraph 4-3",
    "must_cite": "AR 600-8-10"
  },
  {
    "q": "What is the maximum days of ordinary leave a soldier can request without higher approval?",
    "ground_truth": "30 days",
    "must_cite": "AR 600-8-10"
  },
  {
    "q": "What does AR 27-10 say about court-martial convening authority?",
    "ground_truth": "OUT_OF_CORPUS",
    "must_cite": null
  },
  ...
]
```

Write `tests/run_eval.py`:
```python
import json
from adjutant.llm import answer_query
from adjutant.rag import retrieve

with open("tests/eval_questions.json") as f:
    questions = json.load(f)

results = []
for q in questions:
    chunks = retrieve(q["q"], top_k=5)
    answer = answer_query(q["q"], chunks)
    cited_correctly = (
        q["must_cite"] is None
        and "don't have" in answer["spoken_summary"].lower()
    ) or (
        q["must_cite"] is not None
        and any(q["must_cite"] in c["source"] for c in answer["citations"])
    )
    results.append({"q": q["q"], "passed": cited_correctly})

passed = sum(1 for r in results if r["passed"])
print(f"Adjutant: {passed}/{len(results)} ({100*passed/len(results):.0f}%)")
```

Then run the same questions through GenAI.mil's hosted Gemini (via web UI — manually, takes 30 min) and screenshot the hallucinations. **Side-by-side comparison slide.**

This is the single most impressive thing you can build for Mohindra. Don't skip it.

---

## Phase 5 — Sleep (Sun 2:00 AM – 6:00 AM, 4h minimum)

**Non-negotiable.** A demo done by an exhausted founder fails. Set an alarm. Sleep.

If you genuinely have to choose: skip the eval harness and sleep 6 hours instead. Sleep beats polish.

---

## Phase 6 — Demo rehearsal and final polish (Sun 6:00 AM – 1:00 PM, 7h)

### 6.1 First full rehearsal — solo (Sun 6:30 – 7:30 AM, 1h)

Sit at the laptop. Run `docs/DEMO_SCRIPT.md` exactly as written. Time it. **Aim for 4:30, not 5:00 — buffer for nerves.** Note every place you stumbled or the system was slow.

### 6.2 Fix the slow parts (Sun 7:30 – 9:00 AM, 1.5h)

The two most likely slow parts:
- **First transcription is slow** because Whisper loads lazily — pre-warm by running a dummy transcription at server startup (add a `@app.on_event("startup")` handler in `server.py`).
- **First LLM response is slow** because Ollama loads the model lazily — same fix, ping it once at startup.

Open `adjutant/server.py` and add:

```python
@app.on_event("startup")
async def warmup():
    log.info("Warming Whisper…")
    from adjutant.stt import _get_model
    _get_model()
    log.info("Warming Ollama…")
    from adjutant.llm import _client, MODEL
    _client.chat(model=MODEL, messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "hi"}])
    log.info("Warmup done.")
```

This adds 10s to startup but makes every demo response feel instant.

### 6.3 The wifi-disconnect rehearsal (Sun 9:00 – 9:30 AM, 30 min)

Practice the cable yank 5 times. Make sure:
- The browser online/offline badge flips visibly
- The next request still works (it should — but TEST IT)
- You don't accidentally pull the laptop's power cord while pulling the wifi cable
- You can plug it back in cleanly at the end

### 6.4 README final polish + push (Sun 9:30 – 10:30 AM, 1h)

- [ ] README is judge-readable in 60 seconds (already drafted)
- [ ] `git status` clean
- [ ] No secrets in `.env` committed (check `.gitignore`)
- [ ] Repo is **public** (judges will visit)
- [ ] Commit a short demo GIF or screenshot in the README's hero spot
- [ ] Tag a release: `git tag v0.1-scsp && git push --tags`

### 6.5 Friendly stranger demo (Sun 10:30 AM – 12:00 PM, 1.5h)

Find someone in the venue who doesn't know what you've built. Ask: *"Can I demo this for you in 5 minutes? Tell me what's confusing."*

You will discover at least 3 things you didn't notice. Fix them. Re-run.

### 6.6 Buffer + lunch (Sun 12:00 – 1:00 PM, 1h)

If everything works, sit, breathe, eat. **Do not add new features after 12:30 PM Sunday.**

---

## Phase 7 — Submission (Sun 1:00 – 5:00 PM)

### 7.1 Final commit and push (Sun 4:00 PM)
```bash
git add .
git commit -m "Final SCSP submission"
git push origin main
```

### 7.2 Submission email (Sun 5:00 PM HARD DEADLINE)

Subject: `SCSP Hackathon Charlie Mike GenAI.mil`

Body:
```
GitHub link: https://github.com/<username>/adjutant

Attached README.md contents:

[paste README.md inline]
```

Send to `hack@scsp.ai`.

### 7.3 Demo to judges (Sun 5:00 – 7:00 PM)

Run the script. Pull the wifi cable. Smile. Take one breath between beats.

---

## Risk register and what-to-cut-if-behind matrix

| If you're behind by | Cut |
|---|---|
| 1 hour at end of Phase 1 | DA-4856 (counseling form) — go DA-31 + DD-1351-2 only |
| 2 hours at end of Phase 2 | Eval harness comparison — keep the refusal demo, skip the GenAI.mil side-by-side |
| 4 hours at end of Phase 3 | Multi-form-from-one-prompt — go single-form pipeline only |
| 6+ hours at end of Phase 4 | Per-diem math (use static dummy values for the demo) |
| Catastrophic | Drop voice — keep typed input. STT is the most fragile piece; the rest of the pipeline is the actual innovation |

---

## What MUST work for the demo (the floor)

1. Voice input → transcript visible
2. RAG returns at least one cited chunk from AR 600-8-10
3. LLM answer references the source by name (even just *"Per AR 600-8-10, ..."*)
4. ONE filled PDF appears on screen
5. Wifi cable yank moment works (offline indicator flips)
6. Out-of-corpus question demo (the refusal moment)

If those six things work, you are ahead of every team building generic chat over field manuals. Everything else is bonus.

---

## What to prioritize if you have a teammate

- **You** = end-to-end pipeline owner (server.py, prompts, demo script, the pitch)
- **Teammate** = PDF schemas, form-fill plumbing, eval harness
- **Or, if they're frontend** = web/app.js polish, browser audio quirks, online/offline badge UX

Don't both work on the same file simultaneously. Use feature branches if both touching `server.py`.

---

## Your question — Ollama vs Claude for the LLM that powers Adjutant

**Short answer: Ollama, full stop. Claude can't power Adjutant.** Here's why.

### Adjutant's whole pitch dies if it uses Claude

The demo's kill shot is yanking the wifi cable mid-flow and watching it keep working. **Claude is a cloud API** — it requires internet. If Adjutant is calling `api.anthropic.com`, the moment the cable comes out, the demo dies. We lose the entire "voice-first, fully offline" wedge.

That wedge is what differentiates us from:
- **GenAI.mil** (cloud, browser, web-only)
- **Milnerva** (cloud, GPT-4, web-only)
- **Ask Sage** (cloud, IL5 SaaS)
- **CamoGPT** (cloud)

If we go to Claude, we collapse into that lane and lose Novelty (25% of score) and the EdgeRunner positioning ("they do tactical doctrine offline; we do admin offline").

### Mohindra's evaluation rigor specifically rewards local

Dr. Mohindra runs CDAO-funded **AI test & evaluation**. His career is about whether AI is reliable enough for defense use without the cloud round-trip and security review. A demo that says *"runs entirely on this laptop, no external API, no data leaves the device"* is exactly what his research framing values. A demo that calls Claude is one he's seen 100 times.

### What Claude's role actually is in this project

Claude (me, the agent in this terminal) is the **pair coder helping you build Adjutant**. Adjutant itself uses **Llama 3.1 8B via Ollama** as its runtime LLM. Two completely separate roles:

| Tool | Role | Where it runs |
|---|---|---|
| Claude (me) | Helps you write the code, makes architecture calls, drafts prompts | Right now, in this CLI |
| Ollama + Llama 3.1 8B | The brain inside Adjutant when a soldier talks to it | On the laptop, offline, forever |

So the answer to *"should we run this with Ollama and not Claude"* is **yes — Adjutant is already designed that way.** The `ollama` Python client and `llama3.1:8b` model are the runtime; Claude is just the construction crew.

### One subtle nuance worth knowing

There's a hack-the-hackathon move some teams pull: **use Claude during development to debug LLM behavior fast** (prompts iterate quicker against Claude than against local Llama), then swap to Ollama for the demo. We could do that if we hit Llama-specific quality issues during Phase 2.

But the public-facing demo and the submitted code on GitHub must be Ollama. Otherwise the wifi-yank doesn't work and judges see a different repo than what runs on stage.

**Default plan: Ollama all the way through. Claude only as a fallback if we hit an LLM bug we can't fix in 30 min.**

## Claude · 2026-04-25T20:50:02.694Z

# Two parts to the answer — depends on which "they" you mean

## "They" = the SCSP judges grading our demo

**Yes — and they don't even need to.** Here's how it'll actually go:

- **Sunday 5–7 PM:** You walk on stage with **your laptop**. Adjutant runs on your machine, locally. The judges watch you demo it. Llama 3.1 8B is sitting in your laptop's RAM — they see the wifi cable yank, they see it still work, that's the proof.
- **The submission is your GitHub repo URL.** Judges *can* clone it and run it themselves if they're curious — the README has full install steps (`ollama pull llama3.1:8b`, `pip install -r requirements.txt`, etc.). Some judges do this, especially for technical judges like Mohindra. But it's not required for grading.
- **You will NOT run Adjutant on a Pentagon machine for this hackathon.** SCSP rules forbid bringing classified/CAC-gated systems to the event. Demo is on commercial hardware (your laptop), with only public-domain Army PDFs.

## "They" = real soldiers in the future v2 deployment

**This is where it gets interesting and the architecture matters.** Three deployment models, in order of how the project would scale post-hackathon:

### Model 1 — Soldier's own government laptop (most likely)
A junior NCO sits at their unit's NIPR computer (the Windows laptop they already use for paperwork). Adjutant is installed locally on that machine. Llama 3.1 8B runs on the laptop's CPU. Same model we're shipping — just deployed wherever it's needed. **Each laptop is its own copy.**
- Pro: actually offline, no IT approval needed for cloud services
- Pro: works on FOBs with bad connectivity
- Con: every laptop has to download the 5 GB model

### Model 2 — Unit-level edge server (the realistic enterprise pattern)
A single beefy server in each company/battalion runs Llama. Soldiers' laptops just send a voice clip and get a response. The model lives in one place, gets used by 50–200 people in that unit. Same architecture EdgeRunner ships with for SOF deployments.
- Pro: one model download per unit, not per laptop
- Pro: better hardware = faster responses
- Con: needs a real server box; some units don't have one

### Model 3 — On GenAI.mil itself (what the Pentagon would prefer)
Adjutant gets ported to run on the GenAI.mil platform alongside Gemini/ChatGPT/Claude. Soldiers access it through the GenAI.mil web portal with their CAC. Llama runs on GenAI.mil's IL5-certified GPUs.
- Pro: 1.2M users get instant access, no install
- Pro: Pentagon already trusts the security perimeter
- **Con: this kills the offline pitch** — it's back to needing connectivity, which loses the whole reason Adjutant exists for the rank-and-file

### What we tell judges if asked

> *"Adjutant is designed for Model 1 — runs on the soldier's existing government laptop, fully local. Model 2 (unit-level edge) is a natural scale-up for high-density admin offices. Model 3 (GenAI.mil tenant app) is available if connectivity isn't a constraint, but it sacrifices the air-gapped use case the brief calls out — the mechanic in the motor pool, the soldier on the FOB."*

That answer demonstrates we've thought past the hackathon.

---

## TL;DR

- **For the hackathon:** the model runs on YOUR laptop, on stage. Judges watch. They optionally clone the repo to verify.
- **For the future:** soldiers run it on their own government-issued laptops (each laptop a copy), or on a shared unit-level edge server, or as a GenAI.mil tenant app — but Model 1 is the answer that wins this hackathon's framing.

## Naomi · 2026-04-25T20:56:16.815Z

from another claude - 

is this dsn phone angle actually useful? 

Read the Adjutant build, the persona, and the README. Here's the deep answer on phone access — this is actually critical for the pitch because the judges will probe it.

How soldiers/officers actually communicate (and what it means for Adjutant)
The headline: feature phones are essentially nonexistent in the US Army
Unlike the Sabi context (Nigerian feature-phone reality), almost every active-duty US service member carries a personal smartphone. Smartphone penetration among 18-44 year-old Americans is ~95%+, and active-duty military skew higher because median age is 27 and salaries clear the affordability bar. The "basic phone" reality you're used to from Lagos doesn't apply.

But "everyone has a smartphone" doesn't mean "smartphones are usable for Adjutant." There are five distinct device contexts, and they each have different rules.

The five device surfaces in the Army
1. Personal smartphone (BYOD) — the universal device
Every soldier from PVT to GEN owns one. iPhone-heavy among officers, mixed Android/iPhone among enlisted.
Where allowed: barracks, off-duty, MWR areas, dining facility, gym, on-base housing, off-base entirely.
Where banned: SCIFs, classified spaces, many tactical training environments, deployed forward areas, sometimes formations.
Wifi: base wifi (often slow, captive portal), personal hotspot, or cellular on personal plan.
The reality for SGT Chen: her personal iPhone is in her pocket from 5pm to 5am every weekday and all weekend. This is the device she'd actually use.
2. Government-Furnished Equipment (GFE) smartphone — DMUC iPhones/Androids
DoD's DMUC (Department of Defense Mobility Unclassified Capability) manages ~250K+ unclassified mobile devices, mostly iPhones, plus Samsung Knox Androids.
Issued to: officers (typically O-3 and up), senior NCOs in command roles, staff officers, IT/cyber, JAG, recruiters, anyone with a billet that needs mobile email/CAC access.
Junior enlisted (E-1 through E-5) typically do NOT get GFE phones. SGT Chen wouldn't have one.
CAC authentication via Purebred (derived credentials on the device).
App distribution via Apple Business Manager + DoD MDM or Samsung Knox.
This is the "compliance-clean path" for Adjutant on mobile, but it covers the wrong personas — officers who already have desks, not the soldiers in the motor pool.
3. Army-issued laptop on NIPRNet — the desk-bound reality
Standard issue for staff jobs, S-shop work, command teams. Windows 10/11 with HBSS, CAC reader, very locked-down.
This is what GenAI.mil and CamoGPT actually run on today.
For Adjutant: the easiest deployment path. Drop the FastAPI server + Ollama on a base server, expose to NIPRNet, soldiers hit it from any government laptop in S-1.
Problem: this is exactly the "desk-bound" failure mode the SCSP brief is calling out. Doesn't solve the motor-pool case.
4. Tablets (iPads, ruggedized Samsung) — niche but growing
Some maintenance units use ruggedized tablets for IETMs (Interactive Electronic Technical Manuals).
Aviation, intelligence, and special operations have wider tablet adoption.
EdgeRunner specifically targets this — their 8GB-VRAM model runs on a deployed iPad.
Not the right primary surface for Adjutant given the form-fill use case.
5. Landline / DSN phone — the universal-access fallback
Every base has DSN (Defense Switched Network) phones. Every soldier knows their unit's DSN number.
Soldiers without smartphones, or in environments where personal phones are banned, can still dial a DSN number.
This is the Sabi analog. A soldier in a motor pool with no phone access could pick up the shop landline, dial an Adjutant number, talk to it via voice, get the form emailed to their .mil address.
Nobody has built this for Army paperwork. EdgeRunner doesn't. GenAI.mil doesn't. Milnerva doesn't.
What this means for the Adjutant pitch
The judges will ask: "How does SGT Chen actually use this in a motor pool?" You need a clear three-tier deployment story:

Tier 1 — What we built this weekend (the demo)
Laptop with browser mic in barracks rec room (current build). Realistic for SGT Chen tonight after PT. Wifi optional because everything runs offline. This is the persona scenario that's already in docs/PERSONA.md.

Tier 2 — Production v1 (3-6 months)
Mobile-responsive web app accessed from personal smartphone, hitting an instance running on a base NIPRNet server. Soldier opens it on iPhone Safari → uses phone mic → gets PDF emailed to .mil. No app store distribution needed (just a URL behind CAC auth). This is the realistic deployment for 90% of soldiers, because every soldier has a smartphone.

Tier 3 — Production v2 (12-18 months) — the moat extension
DSN voice line. Soldier dials a number from any phone — landline, GFE, personal. Hits the same Adjutant backend via voice, no app needed. This is directly your Sabi pipeline ported to a different telephony stack (Twilio's gov-cloud or AWS Connect for FedRAMP path). Coverage = 100% of soldiers regardless of device, regardless of OPSEC environment. This is what no competitor has and what makes the company defensible.

The slide / Q&A answer to memorize
"Today every soldier has a smartphone — but they're banned in motor pools, classified spaces, and many training environments. GenAI.mil is web-only, desk-only. EdgeRunner runs on tablets that company XOs don't have. We built three deployment surfaces from one backend: a browser app for tonight in the barracks, a mobile web app for the squad leader on her personal phone tomorrow, and a DSN voice line that any soldier with access to any phone can dial — landline, GFE, personal. Same regulation-grounded engine, three surfaces, full coverage of the rank-and-file."

Concrete things I'd add to the build / docs before submission
A 3-line "deployment surfaces" section in the README explaining laptop → mobile web → DSN voice. Right now the README assumes the laptop demo IS the product. That weakens the National Impact score.
A note in PERSONA.md explicitly addressing where SGT Chen's personal smartphone fits in (barracks tonight) versus where she literally cannot use it (PT formation, JRTC field exercise next week).
In the demo Q&A doc, add the question "How does this scale beyond a laptop?" with the three-tier answer above. This will get asked. Mohindra will probe it.
In the limitations section at the bottom of README, replace "30-hour buildable" framing with "30-hour MVP — deployment-surface scaling is the productization roadmap." Signals that you've thought about scale, not just the hackathon.
Bottom line on the question you asked
Army officers/soldiers do NOT use feature phones. Smartphone-universal.
Personal smartphone is the de facto device, but BYOD restrictions limit where Adjutant can run on it.
GFE iPhones (DMUC) are the compliance-clean smartphone path but only cover officers and senior NCOs.
The genuinely novel surface — and your Sabi-stack-extension play — is DSN voice: a phone number any soldier can dial from any device, including dumb landlines in environments where smartphones are banned. That's the only surface that achieves 100% coverage of the rank-and-file the SCSP brief calls out.
Want me to draft the README "Deployment Surfaces" section + an updated DEMO_SCRIPT Q&A entry on this, so the other Claude doesn't have to context-switch?
